    Headline
    laboratory test interference
    General Information
    When interpreting laboratory results, consider laboratory test interference. Drospirenone causes an increase in plasma renin activity and plasma aldosterone induced by its mild anti-mineralocorticoid activity. Estetrol, like other estrogens, may increase the serum concentrations of thyroxine-binding globulin, sex hormone-binding globulin, and cortisol-binding globulin. Use of drospirenone; estetrol, as with other contraceptive steroids, may result in clinical changes that influence the results of certain laboratory tests, such as coagulation factors, lipids, glucose tolerance, and binding proteins.

Description
  Text
  The drospirenone; estetrol product is an oral combined hormonal contraceptive (CHC) used for routine contraception. Drospirenone is a fourth generation progestin and an analog of spironolactone with antimineralocorticoid and antiandrogenic properties. Estetrol (E4) is a synthetic analogue of a native estrogen present during pregnancy. CHCs can be used in female patients from menarche to over the age of 40 years up until the time of menopause with proper selection of products, if no contraindications exist. The choice of a routine contraceptive for any given patient is based on the individual's contraceptive needs, underlying medical conditions or risk factors for adverse effects, and individual preferences for use. All CHCs have risks related to venous and arterial thromboembolism; CHC labels contain a boxed warning regarding the increased risk for thromboembolism in tobacco smokers. The Centers for Disease Control's U.S. Medical Eligibility Criteria describes considerations for risk vs. benefits, including medical conditions or attributes that contraindicate use; these criteria can help practitioners determine product selection for individual patients. Drospirenone may increase potassium levels in some patients; do not use drospirenone; estetrol products in patients with significant risks for hyperkalemia, such as those with kidney, liver, or adrenal insufficiency. Patients taking drugs that could increase serum potassium should consult their health care professional before taking drospirenone-containing oral contraceptive products. Drospirenone; estetrol contraceptives were first FDA-approved in 2021.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Combined hormonal contraceptives prevent pregnancy primarily by suppressing ovulation. Drospirenone is a spironolactone analogue with antimineralocorticoid and antiandrogenic activity. Estetrol is a synthetic analogue of a native estrogen present during pregnancy, that is selective for nuclear estrogen receptor-alpha (ER-alpha) and ER-beta. During clinical trials, a total of 26 on-treatment pregnancies occurred in 1,524 females contributing 12,763 at-risk cycles. The overall Pearl Index was 2.65 (95% CI: 1.73 to 3.88) per 100 woman-years of use.
 
Combination hormonal contraceptives suppress the hypothalamic-pituitary system, decreasing the secretion of gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH). Progestins blunt luteinizing hormone (LH) release, and estrogens suppress follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) from the anterior pituitary. Both estrogen and progestin ultimately inhibit maturation and release of the dominant ovule. In addition, viscosity of the cervical mucus increases with hormonal contraceptive use, which increases the difficulty of sperm entry into the uterus. Alteration in endometrial tissues also occurs, which reduces the likelihood of implantation of the fertilized ovum. The contraceptive effect is reversible. When routine oral contraception is discontinued, ovulation usually returns within three menstrual cycles but can take up to 6 months in some women. Pituitary function and ovarian functions recover more quickly than endometrial activity, which can take up to 3 months to regain normal histology.
 
Both estrogens and progestins are responsible for a number of other metabolic changes in premenopausal women. The summary of these changes is dependent on the net actions of the estrogen and progestin combinations. At the cellular level, estrogens and progestins diffuse into their target cells and interact with a protein receptor. Metabolic responses to estrogens and progestins require an interaction between DNA and the hormone-receptor complex. Target cells include the female reproductive tract, the mammary gland, the hypothalamus, and the pituitary. Estrogens increase the hepatic synthesis of sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG), thyroid-binding globulin (TBG), and other serum proteins. Estrogens generally have a favorable effect on blood lipids; reducing LDL and increasing HDL cholesterol concentrations. Serum triglycerides increase with estrogen administration. Folate metabolism and excretion is increased by estrogens and may lead to slight serum folate deficiency. Estrogens also enhance sodium and fluid retention. Progestins are classified according to their progestational, estrogenic and androgenic properties. Drospirenone has no androgenic, estrogenic, glucocorticoid, or antiglucocorticoid activity. As an analog of spironolactone, the drug exhibits both antimineralocorticoid and antiandrogenic activity. Thus this progestin inhibits testosterone production by the ovaries and adrenal glands, as well as some peripheral testosterone synthesis. Drospirenone's endocrine activity may improve luteal symptoms such as negative mood, water retention, and increased appetite. However, the antimineralocorticoid actions of 3 mg of drospirenone are comparable to 25 mg of spironolactone and may increase serum potassium levels in some patients. Progestins in general can alter hepatic carbohydrate metabolism, increase insulin resistance, and have either little to slightly favorable effects on serum lipoproteins. Less androgenic progestins, like drospirenone, have only slight effects on carbohydrate metabolism and are less likely to aggravate acne. Serious adverse events of hormonal contraceptives, like thrombosis, have long been associated with the estrogen component but may be the result of both estrogen and progestin components. The mechanism for thrombosis may be associated with increased clotting factor production and/or decreases in anti-thrombin III. Minor side effects can be addressed by choosing formulations that take advantage of relative estrogen and progestin potencies.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Following oral administration, the median Tmax is 0.5 hour for estetrol and 1 hour for drospirenone. Administration relative to a meal did not affect the pharmacokinetics (AUC) of the product significantly and the product may be given with or without food.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    The effect of hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of estetrol is unknown. The mean exposure to drospirenone is approximately 3 times higher in females with moderate hepatic impairment than the exposure in females with normal liver function. The effect of severe hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of drospirenone is unknown.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    The effect of renal impairment on the pharmacokinetics of estetrol is unknown. The mean serum drospirenone concentrations increased by 37% in subjects with CrCl of 30 to 49 mL/minute on a low-potassium diet using potassium-sparing drugs. No clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of drospirenone were observed in patients with a CrCl of 50 to 79 mL/minute. Drospirenone treatment did not show any clinically significant effect on serum potassium concentration in the treated patients in this study. Although hyperkalemia was not observed in the study, in 5 of the 7 subjects who continued use of potassium-sparing drugs during the study, mean serum potassium concentrations increased by up to 0.33 mEq/L.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    No clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of estetrol or drospirenone in females were observed based on race/ethnicity (Japanese and Caucasian).
    Name
    Obesity
    Description
    Drospirenone; estetrol may be less effective in females with obesity with a BMI of 30 kg/m2 or higher. A trend of decreasing effectiveness with increasing BMI was observed.
  Text
  Drospirenone; estetrol is administered orally. Estetrol is 46% to 50% bound to plasma protein. Estetrol induces an increase in the serum concentrations of both sex hormone-binding globulin (SHBG) and corticosteroid binding globulin (CBG). While drospirenone is 97% bound to other serum proteins, this progestin does not bind to SHBG or CBG. Estrogens are metabolized in the GI mucosa during absorption and in the liver. The primary metabolic pathway of estetrol is phase 2 metabolism to form glucuronide and sulfate conjugates which have negligible in-vitro estrogenic activity. In vitro studies show that UGT2B7 is the dominant UGT isoform that catalyzes the formation of E4-16-glucuronide. The progestin drospirenone is metabolized to 2 main metabolites, the acid form of drospirenone and the 4,5-dihydrodrospirenone-3-sulfate. The CYP450 microsomal enzymes (i.e., CYP3A4) minimally metabolize drospirenone. At least 20 non-active minor metabolites have been identified. Excretion of both oral contraceptive steroids as inactive metabolites occurs via the urine and feces. The elimination half-life is approximately 34 hours for drospirenone and 27 hours for estetrol at steady state. It is the prolonged biologic effects of the hormones that allow for once-daily administration.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4, UGT2B7
Estetrol (E4): In vitro studies show that UGT2B7 is the dominant UGT isoform that catalyzes the formation of E4-16-glucuronide; however, no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of estetrol were observed when used concomitantly with a UGT2B7 inhibitor. Estetrol is not substrate of CYP1A1, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2D6, 3A4, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, MATE2-K. E4 is unlikely to induce CYP1A2, 2B6, or 3A4 or to inhibit CYP3A4, 1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, D6, 2E1, and is unlikely to inhibit UGT1A9, UGT2B7 or the drug transporters P-glycoprotein (P-gp), BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K at clinically relevant dose.
Drospirenone: Drospirenone is a CYP3A4 substrate. Concomitant use with strong CYP3A inducers or certain moderate or weak CYP3A inducers may decrease drospirenone exposure, which may lead to contraceptive failure. Concomitant use with strong CYP3A inhibitors may increase drospirenone exposure, which may increase the risk of adverse reactions of drospirenone.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
 
Hazardous Drugs Classification
NIOSH 2024 List: Table 1
Observe and exercise appropriate precautions for handling, preparation, administration, and disposal of hazardous drugs.
Use gloves to handle; double chemotherapy gloves and a protective gown are needed if manipulation is necessary. Cutting, crushing, or otherwise manipulating tablets/capsules will increase exposure.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    General Information for Oral Combined Hormonal Contraceptives (CHCs)
Instruct patient on risks and warnings associated with CHCs. Instruct patient to review the manufacturer provided patient information leaflet each time their prescription is filled.
Administer CHC exactly as directed and at intervals not exceeding 24 hours. Take in the order directed in the pack.
28-day package: Take 1 tablet daily as directed by the order of the package. Begin next pack without interruption after the last tablet in the package is taken. The Nextstellis product contains 28 tablets: 24 active hormone tablets and 4 inert tablets. The inert tablets are included so that the daily dosage cycle can be continuous. This reduces the chance of missed doses.
If vomiting or diarrhea occur, another method of contraception may be needed; guidance is available from expert recommendations.
Missed doses: Missed doses can result in spotting or light bleeding and may reduce efficacy. Follow the specific missed dose instructions in the FDA approved patient information leaflet for the specific product. A back-up method of contraception may be needed for 7 days following the missed doses. Guidance for missed CHC doses is also available from the CDC and U.S. Selected Practice Recommendations for Contraceptive Use, including when to consider emergency contraception.
 
General information when initiating routine CHCs:
Initiating CHCs in an individual not previously taking a CHC: There are options to start taking the first cycle of their COC.
Day 1 start: Patient will take the first active tablet of the first pack during the first 24 hours of their menstrual period. The patient will not need to use a back-up method of birth control.
Sunday start: Patient will take the first active tablet of the first pack on the first Sunday after the onset of their menstrual period. When initiating a Sunday start regimen, another method of contraception, such as condoms or spermicide, should be used for the first 7 consecutive days of taking the CHC.
Quick-start: Alternatively, a CHC may be initiated at any time during the menstrual cycle provided the individual is not pregnant. Another method of contraception, such as condoms or spermicide, should be used for the first 7 consecutive days of taking the COC unless the individual abstains from sexual intercourse or the CHC is initiated in the first 5 days of menstrual bleeding.
Switching from a different CHC or other contraceptive methods:
When switching from another combined hormonal contraceptive, start the new oral CHC regimen on the same day that a new pack of the previous oral contraceptive would have been started.
Otherwise, CHCs can usually be started immediately if it is reasonably certain that the person is not pregnant. Waiting for their next menstrual period is unnecessary. If it has been more than 5 days since menstrual bleeding started, the individual needs to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days. Consult specialized recommendations if the patient is switching from an IUD to a CHC.
Initiating a CHC in the person with amenorrhea:
CHCs can be started at any time if it is reasonably certain that the individual is not pregnant. Have the patient abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days.
Initiating CHCs postpartum:
In general, do not initiate in the first 3 weeks postpartum due to thromboembolic risk. If the individual is breast-feeding, do not initiate until after the fourth week postpartum to avoid interference with lactation. Individuals with other risk factors for venous thromboembolism generally should not use CHCs for 3 to 6 weeks (4 to 6 weeks if breast-feeding) after delivery. If the individual starts on a COC postpartum and is less than 6 months postpartum, amenorrheic, and fully or nearly fully breastfeeding (exclusively breastfeeding or the vast majority [85% or more] of feeds are breastfeeds), no additional contraceptive protection is needed. Otherwise, an individual who is 21 days or more days postpartum and has not experienced return of their menstrual cycle needs to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days. If menstrual cycles have returned, and it has been more than 5 days since menstrual bleeding started, they need to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days.
Initiating CHCs following induced or spontaneous abortion:
CHCs may be started at any time postabortion, including immediately after abortion completion or at the time of medication abortion initiation. The patient needs to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days unless CHCs are started at the time of the abortion.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Use of drospirenone; estetrol is contraindicated if hepatic dysfunction is present.
  Renal Impairment
  CrCl less than 80 mL/minute: Use of drospirenone; estetrol is contraindicated.
